ParlaMint-DK_20200121130101	The first item on the agenda is a party leader debate. A year ago we started an experiment with party leader debate in the Chamber, and this is therefore the second party leader debate. The purpose of the trial scheme is to allow party leaders to debate here in the Assembly Chamber without a specific subject being set in advance and without government participation in its capacity as government. Each party leader has three minutes' speaking time from here. The speech is held in the general order of parties, and all party leaders are in the front row. Each speaker is given access to two short remarks of 1⁄2 minutes duration and each time with the same time for replying. Since this is a debate for party leaders, we are not going to use the usual form of indictment, minister or rapporteur, but either Mr. or Mrs. and a name or party leader. That is how I shall give the floor to Mrs Mette Frederiksen.
ParlaMint-DK_20200121130200	On behalf of the group of party leaders, I should like to say that we are endeavouring to comply with the rules in this debate too. I think I can say that on behalf of everyone. I have looked forward to our discussion today and will say that every year, when we turn the calendar page, also represents its anniversaries. This year we will celebrate the hundredth anniversary of South Jutland's return to Denmark. We will also celebrate the 75th anniversary of liberation. These are two very, very happy events. But with it we also mark something else, namely the 75th anniversary of the liberation of the camp in Auschwitz, one of the darkest chapters in human history. I am going to Israel myself tomorrow, where I am going to Israel, among other things. To meet victims of the Holocaust. I also do so from the very sad background that here in Denmark we again see anti-Semitism showing its ugly face. Last year, as everyone here remembers, a Jewish cemetery suffered vandalism, and David's stars were put on mailboxes and house numbers. Like at the time, I think that there is every reason to reject anti-Semitism. We must reject it and we must intervene, and it is therefore our proposal that we should draw up a national action plan against anti-Semitism, with better protection of Jewish institutions and, of course, more information, but also a very clear political disapproval of the anti-Semitism we see in Denmark wherever it comes from. It is only one of many themes that we will be discussing in the year, and I would like to invite all parties to take part in a broad political cooperation, hopefully, and to start by saying thank you for the many different agreements that have already been made cross-cutting. Not least the Climate Act, I would like to emphasise that it is good for future cooperation. Now we have a Climate Act, and then we have to get on with climate action plans, we have to have a multi-year agreement for the police, we have to manage the compensation in the municipal economy, hopefully we have to have a right to a previous pension, we have to follow up on the efforts in relation to foreign fighters and much more, and we have to strengthen the efforts for the most vulnerable children, as reported in the New Year's speech. We do not agree on everything, nor should we be, but I would like to express my thanks for the very positive and constructive approach with which I find a great many parties present themselves for the various negotiations. It impresses me, and I actually think it is an expression of format. I would like to say a special word of thanks to one of the party leaders today, Mr President. Uffe Elbæk, who is today at his last party leader's debate. I have great respect for that vision and the courage that Mr. Uffe Elbæk has deported and brought here to Parliament as party leader. ParlaMint-DK_20200121165613	The draft parliamentary resolution we are debating now, as formulated by the Alternative, is not about banning facial recognition technology or the use of facial recognition technology as such. The aim is to prohibit the use of facial recognition technology by public authorities. I guess you have to read into the fact that private people's use of facial recognition technology is okay. When that is the case, and I agree that it is not the technology itself, that is to say the possibility of doing so, but the use of the one under discussion, we must ask ourselves, as we do in other contexts, what objectives can be achieved that could legitimise the use of this means? What is the point of achieving that makes facial recognition in order to use it? Let me start by stating that I do not share the opinion of the proposers that it is only relevant to look at public authorities. Access to facial recognition technology by private individuals must also reasonably be balanced against the objective which can be achieved. In fact, this is an interesting and important debate, which we have the merit of taking. For the government is the starting point that all Danes deserve to be safe. It is a fundamental condition that the rule of law must provide security to the Danes, otherwise we risk the Danes losing confidence in the rule of law, the cohesion of Denmark disappears. It is right to discuss whether it is possible to talk about the rule of law at all if the state is unable to ensure that the common rules of the game, in the form of laws and other regulations which we have adopted and which apply in society, are also effectively implemented, including, of course, the investigation, investigation and prosecution of crimes. The government protects security so that the Danes can move freely. We take stock of the reality in which we stand and take precautions so that we do not let the criminals undermine what makes Denmark Denmark Denmark. I am therefore, as a starting point, in favour of measures to strengthen the police's capacity to prevent and solve crime, not least because it is part of the foundation of the rule of law. The crime picture is constantly evolving and the authorities must keep up with developments. Face recognition technology is one of several tools that can potentially streamline and facilitate police work. I therefore understand the police's desire to be able to use the technology, and I always listen attentively when the police ask for tools to help achieve it for a legal society that is perfectly legitimate, namely the effective fight against crime. If we do not just embark on facial recognition technology, it is because it also raises some fundamental legal and practical dilemmas which we must not ignore. So this is where we have an objective, we use some funds, but when we use the funds, we also have to consider what the consequences are of using those funds. What could there be of objection to do? Firstly, the use of facial recognition raises fundamental questions about the right to privacy and the security and security that surveillance can provide. Secondly, it must also be said that if we are to move down that road, the use of facial recognition technology by the police must comply with strict legal requirements. It follows from common sense, but it also follows from our international commitments, among others. respect for privacy. This means that the use of facial recognition must be strictly necessary and take place in order to prevent, investigate, detect or prosecute criminal offences or to prevent threats to public security. In addition, issues relating to data protection rules need to be clarified, for example. in relation to the rules on data quality and security. It is important to be aware of this so that any errors in the use of new technology are avoided and the risk of hacking or abuse can also be addressed. It then leads to the third question, namely the practical application: what can technically be done, and how do we prevent the technology from leading to misidentification in this case, of which there have been examples? These are all issues which we need to clarify before they say: let us use this kind of technology. In the view of the government, we need to take tough measures against the criminals who undermine and destroy our free and open societies. I have the expectation that at some point we will discuss facial recognition in more depth. I will not rule out the fact that one day we will be using facial recognition technology, even to a greater extent than what is currently happening, but right now the focus of the government is to implement the initiatives contained in the government's security and security package. It is a very large, ambitious package which contains a great many good initiatives, which provide the police with tools to help solve crimes, among others. by significantly more television surveillance. Against this background, we cannot support the proposal to introduce a ban on facial recognition in public spaces. Thank you for the floor. I look forward to the debate.

ParlaMint-DK_20200121184305	Thank you for the floor. As a member of parliament, this is my first proposal that I am making and we are dealing with today, and I am actually very happy and proud that it is about the rule of law and the right to live freely. In recent years we have seen a worrying development in Denmark which has resulted in legislation that has given the Danes more bans and less freedom. The cover ban, the ghetto package, handshake requirements and the increased use of monitoring and logging are just examples of the fact that in far too many cases the Folketing chooses to override the rights of the individual citizen to freedom and privacy. Organizations and think tanks express concern about developments. For example, Louise Holck, Deputy Director of the Department of Human Rights, said in 2017: • But I can't remember when I last experienced so many examples of fundamental freedoms being disregarded as what we have seen in the past year. ▪ Following the launch of the government's proposal to increase the use of surveillance in the public space in Denmark, the proposers consider it necessary to take the initiative to ensure and strengthen the protection of Danes' freedom, legal certainty and privacy. Denmark is already one of the most monitored countries in the world with 1.5 million cameras. This should not be made worse by also linking software so that citizens can never be at peace from the lurking eye of the state. There is no evidence from research that more monitoring creates more security or that increasing surveillance in public spaces will reduce the severity of crime. On the other hand, developments point to a society in which innovative technology creates a surveillance society that violates our fundamental rights. Despite this, the technological developments of recent years have made facial recognition technology widespread, even in countries with which we are usually comparing ourselves. The Director of the British Data Protection Agency, Elisabeth Denham, recently launched a study in Britain after it was revealed that British police used facial recognition to monitor the population of areas such as King's Cross in London. Similarly, we have recently seen examples from Sweden where the Swedish data controller has known the use of facial recognition on a school illegally. Here it was used to keep track of whether the students had met. In other countries, such as the United States, the United States and Japan. China, facial recognition is used through surveillance cameras on roads, at stations, at airports and at intersections. For example, screens are used at pedestrian crossings to highlight people who have several times crossed the red. The information is combined with other data collected in a single social system which can be accessed if the citizen concerned, for example: wants to borrow money in the bank. The rapid development and implementation of such technology abroad gives cause for concern for an escalated rollout in Denmark, not least after the government has already expressed its desire to increase monitoring in society. The IT-Politics Association has contacted the proposers and informed the Danish Parliament with the adoption of Section 10 of the Law Enforcement Act. 2 and section 7 of the Data Protection Act. 4 and 5 have provided a general legal basis for the police and other public authorities to process sensitive information, including using automatic facial recognition for unique identification of a natural person. The alternative unfortunately finds that this has happened without a public debate on automatic facial recognition and the issues raised by this problematic surveillance technology. In May 2019, the City of San Francisco, California, adopted new legislation including: make it illegal for the authorities, including the police, to use facial recognition technology because it does not work properly and can be misused. Similarly, the European Commission plans to introduce a 5-year ban on the use of facial recognition technology. It is therefore proposed to introduce a general ban on the implementation and use of facial recognition technology by public authorities for surveillance in public spaces, unless this is done with the consent of the citizen. The alternative is clear that the authorities should not, as a starting point, have the tools to recognise citizens through constant invasive surveillance in public spaces. We are a rule of law, not a paranoid banana republic that ruthlessly monitors its citizens and steals their freedom. Thank you very much for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20200121185935	The debate is open. The first to speak is the Minister of Justice. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20200121185957	Thank you for the floor and also thank you to the Danish People's Party for tabling the motion for a resolution banning the change of names for criminals. This is a debate which we began as early as November last year, and the motion for a resolution instructs the government to put forward a draft law prohibiting people sentenced to more than three months' unconditional imprisonment from changing their names while the sentence is shown in their criminal records. The authors point out, on the one hand, that access to a change of name increases the risk of a convicted person committing new crime and, on the other hand, that it will be possible for a citizen who has been contacted by an unknown citizen to ascertain whether he or she has committed serious crime by following the media. The aim is to ensure the safety of citizens. I believe that it is my prime task as Minister of Justice to ensure the safety of our citizens. Security is a prerequisite for a good life. We all deserve to be safe. I therefore fully agree with the authors of the motion for a resolution that the security of citizens must be guaranteed. The big and difficult question is then how to ensure the safety of citizens. When a resolution like this lands on my table, there is reason to ask itself two things. One is: Do the police believe that this can prevent crime or help during an investigation? The second is: Will it give the individual citizen greater security in everyday life? If we now start with the police, the National Police have announced that it is the CPR number and not the name that is crucial to the police and, as you know, you cannot change the CPR number. A name change recorded in the CPR number of a CPR registry is automatically updated in the Crime Register, which means that the police always have the opportunity to look up whether a person has committed crime in the past, even if they have changed their name. In other words, a change of name will not affect the work of the police. What about the citizen? In other words, if we are to be completely honest, it is difficult to see how a ban on changing names can really make a difference to the security of our citizens. To a very large extent, these will be only the names of criminals who have been the subject of very extensive media coverage that citizens will recognise. And my claim will be that even some of those who have been the subject of a lot of media talk cannot be remembered in the general public, including those of us who are here today. And if we are to be completely honest, we must also admit that the ban on the change of names does not prevent the convicted criminal from introducing himself with another name when he or she, for example. contact new neighbours or otherwise contact other people with or without criminal intent. I've ended up where I think it almost creates a false sense of security. There is no doubt, however, that a case such as that referred to by the authors of the motion for a resolution concerning the three killings of elderly people on Nørrebro is deeply tragic and that, of course, it gives rise to the question of whether there are steps we can take to ensure that this does not happen again. Unfortunately, in my view, a ban on changing names is not the answer. Nor is it a means sought by the police in terms of prevention. I believe that this is crucial to our security, among other things. is that we have an accessible and effective police force and that we politicians have an important task in ensuring that the police have the tools that the police need to look after us, according to the discussion we have just had. The government is also focusing on this. Shortly before Christmas we had among other things. together with the authors of the motion for a resolution, a proposal that we adopted to prevent convicted criminals from committing new acts of violence. The law provides, among other things: the right of the courts to impose a ban on sexual offenders in certain places where there is a danger that the convicted person may commit new acts of violence. It can be certain sports halls, it can be playgrounds, it can be swimming halls. In addition, the courts can already ban convicted sex offenders from contacting children. With the law, we introduced enhanced supervision of all these relationships. The police can therefore, in the future, carry out unannounced supervision of convicted sex offenders who have been banned from staying in certain places and a ban on contact with children. In short, the legislation has ensured that the ban does not only exist on paper but also works out in reality. The Folketing will soon discuss several of the government's proposals to ensure security in the public domain. Here I would like to highlight the proposal to increase the use of television surveillance. Among other things, the police must: have the opportunity to set up another 300 cameras that the police can use in the fight against crime. The police must also be able to take over and use television surveillance by other public and private authorities. Those tools are of great importance for police crime prevention and investigation. These are quite specific measures that the government has taken and, for me, they are far more effective in the ambition to prevent new violations than to ban name-changes. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution, but I think there is reason to thank the authors of the motion for a resolution for putting the subject on the agenda, because we must constantly keep the Danes' security focused on ensuring. ParlaMint-DK_20200121195051	Thank you. Hopefully, this can help to prevent abuse, violence, theft against children, young people and adults living in housing, in residences, in 24-hour institutions and foster families. In addition to this first element of the draft law with criminal records, we also make it clear that the approval and supervision of the various types of social services by the Social Inspectorate includes the total effort provided by the offer. This means that, in future, supervision must also be exercised, for example. relief under Section 84 of the Service Act when it takes place in an offer approved by the Social Inspectorate. In such offers, therefore, there will no longer be citizens who, so to speak, are outside the supervision. Finally, as the third and also very important element of the legislative proposal, we are making it easier for funds operating social services to change their purpose clause so that they are better able to develop offers according to needs and new knowledge. There have been some examples of the fact that, under the current rules, funds have not been able to change their purpose, although it has seemed quite obvious that it would benefit residents at all. That is why it is good that we should make the rules more flexible. All in all, then, it is a good bill, which the Social Democrats naturally support.

ParlaMint-DK_20200121200002	Thank you for that, President. On a public record, decisions will remain valid for 10 years, while on a private record they will remain valid for 2, 3 or 5 years, depending on the penalty involved.
